========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, I guess if I really think about it... no, it's not a brand new thing. Like, looking back at elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards all the time that I was a bright kid, but I was just constantly losing my worksheets. 

I remember this one time in fourth grade, Mr. Henderson's class—he was obsessed with dinosaurs and had this huge T-Rex skull replica on his desk that we weren't allowed to touch. Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. He bought it at a museum gift shop on a road trip, I think. I love road trips, actually, except for the fast food. You eat burgers for three days straight and you just feel terrible. 

But anyway, I'd always lose his spelling packets, or I'd just completely miss the instructions when he was explaining what we were supposed to do. My mom actually tried color-coding my folders to help me keep track of things. Red for math, blue for reading... I think green was science? Or maybe green was social studies. I always hated social studies because we had to memorize all the state capitals, and I still don't know why I need to know the capital of North Dakota. Bismarck, maybe? 

But yeah, my teachers always had to come over to my desk and give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork instead of just sitting there. So I guess the missing instructions and needing someone to remind me to finish my work has kind of always been a thing.

Um, yeah, sitting still is definitely a struggle. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. Like, looking back at elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards all the time that I was a bright kid, but I was just constantly losing my worksheets. \n\nI remember this one time in fourth grade, Mr. Henderson's class—he was obsessed with dinosaurs and had this huge T-Rex skull replica on his desk that we weren't allowed to touch. Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. He bought it at a museum gift shop on a road trip, I think. I love road trips, actually, except for the fast food. You eat burgers for three days straight and you just feel terrible. \n\nBut anyway, I'd always lose his spelling packets, or I'd just completely miss the instructions when he was explaining what we were supposed to do. My mom actually tried color-coding my folders to help me keep track of things. Red for math, blue for reading... I think green was science? Or maybe green was social studies. I always hated social studies because we had to memorize all the state capitals, and I still don't know why I need to know the capital of North Dakota. Bismarck, maybe? \n\nBut yeah, my teachers always had to come over to my desk and give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork instead of just sitting there. So I guess the missing instructions and needing someone to remind me to finish my work has kind of always been a thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides getting up from your desk at work, do you find it hard to sit still in general? Like, when you're watching TV or in meetings, do you feel like you need to be moving or fidgeting with something?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, sitting still is definitely a struggle. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. Or just constantly shifting around in my chair, even when I'm really trying to look like I'm paying attention. The chairs in our conference room are the worst, by the way. They have this mesh back that's supposed to be ergonomic or whatever, but it just pinches you if you lean back too far. I don't know who picked them out, probably the same person who decided the second floor was a good place for our department. \n\nI actually tried bringing in my own cushion once, one of those memory foam ones? But I left it in the breakroom by accident and when I went back for it, it was gone. Who steals a seat cushion? It's just weird. It reminds me of when someone kept stealing my yogurts from the fridge at my old apartment. It was my roommate's boyfriend, I'm pretty sure, because he was always there and he ate constantly. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, clicking pens, bouncing my leg, shifting around... I do all that stuff pretty much anytime I have to sit in a meeting or a training for more than a few minutes.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were in elementary school, like in first, second, or third grade, do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your attention or activity level back then, or did those issues really only start showing up later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, like I was saying before, it definitely wasn't a brand new thing that just popped up later on. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. They'd always write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was constantly losing my worksheets or missing instructions. 

I actually still have some of those old report cards in a box somewhere at my parents' house. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. I don't really blame him for being angry if he kept getting stuck in a trap in our attic. 

But yeah, anyway... the teachers were definitely concerned back then. They always had to give me reminders to actually finish my classwork because I'd just be sitting there not doing it. So it's definitely been going on since I was little.

Um, no, I've never actually been evaluated for anything like this before. No doctor or anyone has ever given me a diagnosis or anything. I mean, my mom used to joke about it when I was younger, but we never actually went to a doctor. 

Honestly, going to the doctor was always a huge ordeal when I was a kid anyway. My pediatrician's office was in this massive medical complex, and the parking garage was basically a labyrinth. My mom would always lose the ticket, or forget what level we parked on. Once, we spent like forty-five minutes wandering around level four, looking for our minivan. It was a Dodge Caravan, one of those weird teal colors they used to make in the 90s. We called it the "Teal Turtle" because it was so incredibly slow going uphill. We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. My sister got carsick on that trip, actually. She always got carsick, even on short trips to the grocery store. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. 

But yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.

Anyway, my supervisor is starting to notice I'm not at my desk when I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your desk trying to do your work, do you find it hard to keep your attention on the task itself, or is it more that you feel restless and need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's more that my attention just... slides right off what I'm doing. Like, I'll be staring at a file, and if the people in the next cubicle start talking about another claim, or if I just suddenly remember some random errand I have to do after work... like picking up my dry cleaning. Actually, I need to find a new dry cleaner because the one I go to shrunk my favorite sweater last week. It was this really nice wool one my mom got me for Christmas, and now it could probably fit a toddler. It's such a hassle to find a place that actually reads the care labels, you know? But anyway, yeah, I'll get distracted by a thought like that, or my coworkers talking, and my focus is just gone. I end up having to reread the exact same section of the claim a few times before it actually registers in my brain.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with attention and getting up from your desk? Have they been going on for most of your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, well, the work stuff specifically... that really started about 15 months ago? Yeah, because that’s when they did this huge department reorganization. They moved our whole team from the third floor down to the second floor, which honestly sucks because the second floor doesn't get nearly as much natural light. I used to have this little potted plant on my desk—a pothos, I think? My roommate gave it to me when we first signed our lease. \n\nHe’s actually moving out next month, though, because his girlfriend got a job in Seattle, so now I have to find a new roommate. It is just the absolute worst process. People on the internet are so weird, you know? Like, one guy asked if I was okay with him practicing his bagpipes at 6 AM. I can barely wake up to my normal alarm, let alone bagpipes. Anyway, the plant completely died because of the new office lighting. \n\nBut wait, what was I... oh, right. Yeah, ever since that reorganization 15 months ago, that's when I really started noticing how bad I was getting with the zoning out and wandering away from my desk.\n\nDoctor: Before that reorganization 15 months ago, did you have trouble focusing or staying in your seat at work, or in school before that? Or is this really the first time you've dealt with this kind of thing?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess if I really think about it... no, it's not a brand new thing. Like, looking back at elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards all the time that I was a bright kid, but I was just constantly losing my worksheets. \n\nI remember this one time in fourth grade, Mr. Henderson's class—he was obsessed with dinosaurs and had this huge T-Rex skull replica on his desk that we weren't allowed to touch. Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. He bought it at a museum gift shop on a road trip, I think. I love road trips, actually, except for the fast food. You eat burgers for three days straight and you just feel terrible. \n\nBut anyway, I'd always lose his spelling packets, or I'd just completely miss the instructions when he was explaining what we were supposed to do. My mom actually tried color-coding my folders to help me keep track of things. Red for math, blue for reading... I think green was science? Or maybe green was social studies. I always hated social studies because we had to memorize all the state capitals, and I still don't know why I need to know the capital of North Dakota. Bismarck, maybe? \n\nBut yeah, my teachers always had to come over to my desk and give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork instead of just sitting there. So I guess the missing instructions and needing someone to remind me to finish my work has kind of always been a thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides getting up from your desk at work, do you find it hard to sit still in general? Like, when you're watching TV or in meetings, do you feel like you need to be moving or fidgeting with something?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, sitting still is definitely a struggle. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. Or just constantly shifting around in my chair, even when I'm really trying to look like I'm paying attention. The chairs in our conference room are the worst, by the way. They have this mesh back that's supposed to be ergonomic or whatever, but it just pinches you if you lean back too far. I don't know who picked them out, probably the same person who decided the second floor was a good place for our department. \n\nI actually tried bringing in my own cushion once, one of those memory foam ones? But I left it in the breakroom by accident and when I went back for it, it was gone. Who steals a seat cushion? It's just weird. It reminds me of when someone kept stealing my yogurts from the fridge at my old apartment. It was my roommate's boyfriend, I'm pretty sure, because he was always there and he ate constantly. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, clicking pens, bouncing my leg, shifting around... I do all that stuff pretty much anytime I have to sit in a meeting or a training for more than a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, like in first, second, or third grade, do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your attention or activity level back then, or did those issues really only start showing up later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Um, well, like I was saying before, it definitely wasn't a brand new thing that just popped up later on. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. They'd always write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was constantly losing my worksheets or missing instructions. \n\nI actually still have some of those old report cards in a box somewhere at my parents' house. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. I don't really blame him for being angry if he kept getting stuck in a trap in our attic. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the teachers were definitely concerned back then. They always had to give me reminders to actually finish my classwork because I'd just be sitting there not doing it. So it's definitely been going on since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your whole life—school, work, relationships, hobbies—have these attention and fidgeting issues actually caused real problems for you, or have you mostly been able to manage okay despite them?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, they've definitely caused real problems. Like right now, at work, it's actually getting pretty bad. My supervisor just put me on this performance coaching plan because my claim files are falling way behind. And she's questioning my accuracy, which is super frustrating because I'm usually pretty good at catching details, just... not lately, I guess. \n\nIt really sucks because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I'm actually worried I might lose that opportunity completely. Senior adjusters get their own cubicles by the windows, which is huge because right now I'm in the middle row where the fluorescent lights just buzz all day. It's this high-pitched hum that nobody else seems to hear, but it drives me crazy. I brought it up to maintenance, and this guy Gary came up to check it. Gary's nice, he always wears these vintage bowling shirts, like every single day. I asked him where he gets them, and he said thrift stores, mostly, but you have to go on Tuesdays when they put the new stock out. I tried going to a thrift store on a Tuesday once, but I ended up just buying a bunch of old vinyl records I don't even have a player for. I just liked the album covers. The artwork from the 70s is so weird and cool, you know? Like, there's always a wizard or a spaceship or something. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the performance coaching thing. It's definitely a problem right now. If I don't get my numbers up and fix the accuracy issues, I don't know what's going to happen with the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist before for attention problems or ADHD, or has anyone ever given you a diagnosis related to your attention or activity level?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I've never actually been evaluated for anything like this before. No doctor or anyone has ever given me a diagnosis or anything. I mean, my mom used to joke about it when I was younger, but we never actually went to a doctor. \n\nHonestly, going to the doctor was always a huge ordeal when I was a kid anyway. My pediatrician's office was in this massive medical complex, and the parking garage was basically a labyrinth. My mom would always lose the ticket, or forget what level we parked on. Once, we spent like forty-five minutes wandering around level four, looking for our minivan. It was a Dodge Caravan, one of those weird teal colors they used to make in the 90s. We called it the \"Teal Turtle\" because it was so incredibly slow going uphill. We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. My sister got carsick on that trip, actually. She always got carsick, even on short trips to the grocery store. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides the work performance issues you mentioned, have these attention and fidgeting problems caused you real difficulties in other areas of your life—like in your relationships, your social life, or managing everyday tasks at home?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Um, well, let's see... relationships and everyday tasks... I mean, I don't really have a lot to say about that. But I left it in the breakroom by accident and when I went back for it, it was gone. Who steals a seat cushion? It's just weird. It reminds me of when someone kept stealing my yogurts from the fridge at my old apartment. It was my roommate's boyfriend, I'm pretty sure, because he was always there and he ate constantly. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, clicking pens, bouncing my leg, shifting around... I do all that stuff pretty much anytime I have to sit in a meeting or a training for more than a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, like in first, second, or third grade, do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your attention or activity level back then, or did those issues really only start showing up later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Um, well, like I was saying before, it definitely wasn't a brand new thing that just popped up later on. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. They'd always write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was constantly losing my worksheets or missing instructions. \n\nI actually still have some of those old report cards in a box somewhere at my parents' house. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. I don't really blame him for being angry if he kept getting stuck in a trap in our attic. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the teachers were definitely concerned back then. They always had to give me reminders to actually finish my classwork because I'd just be sitting there not doing it. So it's definitely been going on since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your whole life—school, work, relationships, hobbies—have these attention and fidgeting issues actually caused real problems for you, or have you mostly been able to manage okay despite them?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, they've definitely caused real problems. Like right now, at work, it's actually getting pretty bad. My supervisor just put me on this performance coaching plan because my claim files are falling way behind. And she's questioning my accuracy, which is super frustrating because I'm usually pretty good at catching details, just... not lately, I guess. \n\nIt really sucks because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I'm actually worried I might lose that opportunity completely. Senior adjusters get their own cubicles by the windows, which is huge because right now I'm in the middle row where the fluorescent lights just buzz all day. It's this high-pitched hum that nobody else seems to hear, but it drives me crazy. I brought it up to maintenance, and this guy Gary came up to check it. Gary's nice, he always wears these vintage bowling shirts, like every single day. I asked him where he gets them, and he said thrift stores, mostly, but you have to go on Tuesdays when they put the new stock out. I tried going to a thrift store on a Tuesday once, but I ended up just buying a bunch of old vinyl records I don't even have a player for. I just liked the album covers. The artwork from the 70s is so weird and cool, you know? Like, there's always a wizard or a spaceship or something. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the performance coaching thing. It's definitely a problem right now. If I don't get my numbers up and fix the accuracy issues, I don't know what's going to happen with the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist before for attention problems or ADHD, or has anyone ever given you a diagnosis related to your attention or activity level?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I've never actually been evaluated for anything like this before. No doctor or anyone has ever given me a diagnosis or anything. I mean, my mom used to joke about it when I was younger, but we never actually went to a doctor. \n\nHonestly, going to the doctor was always a huge ordeal when I was a kid anyway. My pediatrician's office was in this massive medical complex, and the parking garage was basically a labyrinth. My mom would always lose the ticket, or forget what level we parked on. Once, we spent like forty-five minutes wandering around level four, looking for our minivan. It was a Dodge Caravan, one of those weird teal colors they used to make in the 90s. We called it the \"Teal Turtle\" because it was so incredibly slow going uphill. We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. My sister got carsick on that trip, actually. She always got carsick, even on short trips to the grocery store. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the work performance issues you mentioned, have these attention and fidgeting problems caused you real difficulties in other areas of your life—like in your relationships, your social life, or managing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Um, well, let's see... relationships and everyday tasks... I mean, I don't really have a lot to say about that. I haven't really noticed it causing huge problems with my friends or at home, or at least I'm not sure if it does. I mostly just hang out with a couple of guys from high school anyway. We usually play video games online on Thursday nights. We've been playing this one game, Destiny 2, for like years now. It's crazy how much money they expect you to spend on expansions, though. Every time you turn around, it's another forty bucks just to get the new weapons and the new storyline. And the storyline doesn't even make sense half the time anymore. They killed off my favorite character a few years ago and I'm still kind of mad about it. His name was Cayde-6, he was this robot guy with a horn on his head, and he was hilarious. The guy who voiced him is that actor from Firefly, Nathan Fillion. I loved Firefly. He always rushes in and dies immediately, and then we have to revive him while getting shot at. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, I haven't really noticed major problems outside of work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, paying bills and stuff... I mean, mostly that's okay, I guess? Everything's on autopay now, which is a lifesaver. But honestly, the forgetting stuff happens way more at work than at home. Like, I forget simple follow-ups way more than I want to admit. I'll get off the phone with a claimant and mean to return their call later with an answer, or I need to update a diary note on a file, or send out a form I told them I'd send... and it just totally slips my mind. 

It's like I have the thought, and then it's just gone. Like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there. My apartment layout makes that happen all the time. It's this weird shotgun style where you have to walk through the bedroom to get to the bathroom, and the kitchen is sort of just a wall in the living room. The fridge is right next to the front door, which makes bringing groceries in easy, but it's super loud. It makes this weird clunking noise right before the compressor kicks on. I actually tried to record the sound once to play for the landlord, but my phone microphone wouldn't pick it up right. I have one of those older phones, the ones with the physical home button still. I refuse to upgrade because I hate the idea of not having a real button to press. Plus, the new ones don't even come with a charger in the box anymore, which feels like a total scam. You pay a thousand dollars for a phone and they can't even give you a little plastic brick to plug it into the wall? It's ridiculous. 

But yeah, work is where the forgetting really gets me in trouble. The follow-ups and the forms and stuff. At home, I don't really have claimants waiting on me to send them things, so it's not as big of a deal.

I used to have this little potted plant on my desk—a pothos, I think? My roommate gave it to me when we first signed our lease. \n\nHe’s actually moving out next month, though, because his girlfriend got a job in Seattle, so now I have to find a new roommate. It is just the absolute worst process. People on the internet are so weird, you know? Like, one guy asked if I was okay with him practicing his bagpipes at 6 AM. I can barely wake up to my normal alarm, let alone bagpipes. Anyway, the plant completely died because of the new office lighting. \n\nBut wait, what was I... oh, right. Yeah, ever since that reorganization 15 months ago, that's when I really started noticing how bad I was getting with the zoning out and wandering away from my desk.\n\nDoctor: Before that reorganization 15 months ago, did you have trouble focusing or staying in your seat at work, or in school before that? Or is this really the first time you've dealt with this kind of thing?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess if I really think about it... no, it's not a brand new thing. Like, looking back at elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards all the time that I was a bright kid, but I was just constantly losing my worksheets. \n\nI remember this one time in fourth grade, Mr. Henderson's class—he was obsessed with dinosaurs and had this huge T-Rex skull replica on his desk that we weren't allowed to touch. Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. He bought it at a museum gift shop on a road trip, I think. I love road trips, actually, except for the fast food. You eat burgers for three days straight and you just feel terrible. \n\nBut anyway, I'd always lose his spelling packets, or I'd just completely miss the instructions when he was explaining what we were supposed to do. My mom actually tried color-coding my folders to help me keep track of things. Red for math, blue for reading... I think green was science? Or maybe green was social studies. I always hated social studies because we had to memorize all the state capitals, and I still don't know why I need to know the capital of North Dakota. Bismarck, maybe? \n\nBut yeah, my teachers always had to come over to my desk and give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork instead of just sitting there. So I guess the missing instructions and needing someone to remind me to finish my work has kind of always been a thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides getting up from your desk at work, do you find it hard to sit still in general? Like, when you're watching TV or in meetings, do you feel like you need to be moving or fidgeting with something?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, sitting still is definitely a struggle. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. Or just constantly shifting around in my chair, even when I'm really trying to look like I'm paying attention. The chairs in our conference room are the worst, by the way. They have this mesh back that's supposed to be ergonomic or whatever, but it just pinches you if you lean back too far. I don't know who picked them out, probably the same person who decided the second floor was a good place for our department. \n\nI actually tried bringing in my own cushion once, one of those memory foam ones? But I left it in the breakroom by accident and when I went back for it, it was gone. Who steals a seat cushion? It's just weird. It reminds me of when someone kept stealing my yogurts from the fridge at my old apartment. It was my roommate's boyfriend, I'm pretty sure, because he was always there and he ate constantly. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, clicking pens, bouncing my leg, shifting around... I do all that stuff pretty much anytime I have to sit in a meeting or a training for more than a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, like in first, second, or third grade, do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your attention or activity level back then, or did those issues really only start showing up later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Um, well, like I was saying before, it definitely wasn't a brand new thing that just popped up later on. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. They'd always write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was constantly losing my worksheets or missing instructions. \n\nI actually still have some of those old report cards in a box somewhere at my parents' house. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. I don't really blame him for being angry if he kept getting stuck in a trap in our attic. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the teachers were definitely concerned back then. They always had to give me reminders to actually finish my classwork because I'd just be sitting there not doing it. So it's definitely been going on since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your whole life—school, work, relationships, hobbies—have these attention and fidgeting issues actually caused real problems for you, or have you mostly been able to manage okay despite them?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, they've definitely caused real problems. Like right now, at work, it's actually getting pretty bad. My supervisor just put me on this performance coaching plan because my claim files are falling way behind. And she's questioning my accuracy, which is super frustrating because I'm usually pretty good at catching details, just... not lately, I guess. \n\nIt really sucks because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I'm actually worried I might lose that opportunity completely. Senior adjusters get their own cubicles by the windows, which is huge because right now I'm in the middle row where the fluorescent lights just buzz all day. It's this high-pitched hum that nobody else seems to hear, but it drives me crazy. I brought it up to maintenance, and this guy Gary came up to check it. Gary's nice, he always wears these vintage bowling shirts, like every single day. I asked him where he gets them, and he said thrift stores, mostly, but you have to go on Tuesdays when they put the new stock out. I tried going to a thrift store on a Tuesday once, but I ended up just buying a bunch of old vinyl records I don't even have a player for. I just liked the album covers. The artwork from the 70s is so weird and cool, you know? Like, there's always a wizard or a spaceship or something. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the performance coaching thing. It's definitely a problem right now. If I don't get my numbers up and fix the accuracy issues, I don't know what's going to happen with the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist before for attention problems or ADHD, or has anyone ever given you a diagnosis related to your attention or activity level?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I've never actually been evaluated for anything like this before. No doctor or anyone has ever given me a diagnosis or anything. I mean, my mom used to joke about it when I was younger, but we never actually went to a doctor. \n\nHonestly, going to the doctor was always a huge ordeal when I was a kid anyway. My pediatrician's office was in this massive medical complex, and the parking garage was basically a labyrinth. My mom would always lose the ticket, or forget what level we parked on. Once, we spent like forty-five minutes wandering around level four, looking for our minivan. It was a Dodge Caravan, one of those weird teal colors they used to make in the 90s. We called it the \"Teal Turtle\" because it was so incredibly slow going uphill. We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. My sister got carsick on that trip, actually. She always got carsick, even on short trips to the grocery store. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the work performance issues you mentioned, have these attention and fidgeting problems caused you real difficulties in other areas of your life—like in your relationships, your social life, or managing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Um, well, let's see... relationships and everyday tasks... I mean, I don't really have a lot to say about that. I haven't really noticed it causing huge problems with my friends or at home, or at least I'm not sure if it does. I mostly just hang out with a couple of guys from high school anyway. We usually play video games online on Thursday nights. We've been playing this one game, Destiny 2, for like years now. It's crazy how much money they expect you to spend on expansions, though. Every time you turn around, it's another forty bucks just to get the new weapons and the new storyline. And the storyline doesn't even make sense half the time anymore. They killed off my favorite character a few years ago and I'm still kind of mad about it. His name was Cayde-6, he was this robot guy with a horn on his head, and he was hilarious. The guy who voiced him is that actor from Firefly, Nathan Fillion. I loved Firefly. It's a crime they cancelled it after one season. My buddy Dave actually has a replica of the spaceship from that show sitting on his desk. Dave's a cool guy, but he's terrible at Destiny. He always rushes in and dies immediately, and then we have to revive him while getting shot at. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, I haven't really noticed major problems outside of work. Maybe I just don't pay enough attention to it, but I don't really have anything specific to tell you about that stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think about managing things at home—like paying bills on time, keeping your apartment clean, remembering appointments, or keeping up with responsibilities—do you find yourself struggling with those kinds of things the way you struggle at work, or do those seem to go more smoothly for you?\n\nPatient: Um, paying bills and stuff... I mean, mostly that's okay, I guess? Everything's on autopay now, which is a lifesaver. But honestly, the forgetting stuff happens way more at work than at home. Like, I forget simple follow-ups way more than I want to admit. I'll get off the phone with a claimant and mean to return their call later with an answer, or I need to update a diary note on a file, or send out a form I told them I'd send... and it just totally slips my mind. \n\nIt's like I have the thought, and then it's just gone. Like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there. My apartment layout makes that happen all the time. It's this weird shotgun style where you have to walk through the bedroom to get to the bathroom, and the kitchen is sort of just a wall in the living room. The fridge is right next to the front door, which makes bringing groceries in easy, but it's super loud. It makes this weird clunking noise right before the compressor kicks on. I actually tried to record the sound once to play for the landlord, but my phone microphone wouldn't pick it up right. I have one of those older phones, the ones with the physical home button still. I refuse to upgrade because I hate the idea of not having a real button to press. Plus, the new ones don't even come with a charger in the box anymore, which feels like a total scam. You pay a thousand dollars for a phone and they can't even give you a little plastic brick to plug it into the wall? It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, work is where the forgetting really gets me in trouble. The follow-ups and the forms and stuff. At home, I don't really have claimants waiting on me to send them things, so it's not as big of a deal.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, well, looking back, I guess it's not exactly a new thing for me, honestly. Even way before high school, like back in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I was always losing my worksheets or missing instructions. They were constantly having to remind me to finish my classwork. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was always getting on me about my messy desk. Her classroom always smelled strongly of peppermint, because she kept this huge glass jar of peppermints on her desk. She said they helped with digestion, but I think she just really liked them. She'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I was terrible at spelling, though. I could never remember if it was "i before e" or "e before i." English has so many weird rules that contradict themselves. Like, why is "colonel" spelled with an "l" but pronounced with an "r"? It makes no sense. I tried looking it up once, and it has something to do with the French and the Italians arguing over military terms hundreds of years ago. History is full of stuff like that, just people arguing and messing things up for the rest of us. 

But yeah, I guess I've always kind of struggled with missing things and needing reminders, even back then. High school wasn't much different, really.

Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. It's this high-pitched hum that nobody else seems to hear, but it drives me crazy. I brought it up to maintenance, and this guy Gary came up to check it. Gary's nice, he always wears these vintage bowling shirts, like every single day. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the work performance issues you mentioned, have these attention and fidgeting problems caused you real difficulties in other areas of your life—like in your relationships, your social life, or managing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Um, well, let's see... relationships and everyday tasks... I mean, I don't really have a lot to say about that. I haven't really noticed it causing huge problems with my friends or at home, or at least I'm not sure if it does. I mostly just hang out with a couple of guys from high school anyway. We usually play video games online on Thursday nights. We've been playing this one game, Destiny 2, for like years now. It's crazy how much money they expect you to spend on expansions, though. Every time you turn around, it's another forty bucks just to get the new weapons and the new storyline. And the storyline doesn't even make sense half the time anymore. They killed off my favorite character a few years ago and I'm still kind of mad about it. His name was Cayde-6, he was this robot guy with a horn on his head, and he was hilarious. The guy who voiced him is that actor from Firefly, Nathan Fillion. I loved Firefly. It's a crime they cancelled it after one season. My buddy Dave actually has a replica of the spaceship from that show sitting on his desk. Dave's a cool guy, but he's terrible at Destiny. He always rushes in and dies immediately, and then we have to revive him while getting shot at. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, I haven't really noticed major problems outside of work. Maybe I just don't pay enough attention to it, but I don't really have anything specific to tell you about that stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think about managing things at home—like paying bills on time, keeping your apartment clean, remembering appointments, or keeping up with responsibilities—do you find yourself struggling with those kinds of things the way you struggle at work, or do those seem to go more smoothly for you?\n\nPatient: Um, paying bills and stuff... I mean, mostly that's okay, I guess? Everything's on autopay now, which is a lifesaver. But honestly, the forgetting stuff happens way more at work than at home. Like, I forget simple follow-ups way more than I want to admit. I'll get off the phone with a claimant and mean to return their call later with an answer, or I need to update a diary note on a file, or send out a form I told them I'd send... and it just totally slips my mind. \n\nIt's like I have the thought, and then it's just gone. Like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there. My apartment layout makes that happen all the time. It's this weird shotgun style where you have to walk through the bedroom to get to the bathroom, and the kitchen is sort of just a wall in the living room. The fridge is right next to the front door, which makes bringing groceries in easy, but it's super loud. It makes this weird clunking noise right before the compressor kicks on. I actually tried to record the sound once to play for the landlord, but my phone microphone wouldn't pick it up right. I have one of those older phones, the ones with the physical home button still. I refuse to upgrade because I hate the idea of not having a real button to press. Plus, the new ones don't even come with a charger in the box anymore, which feels like a total scam. You pay a thousand dollars for a phone and they can't even give you a little plastic brick to plug it into the wall? It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, work is where the forgetting really gets me in trouble. The follow-ups and the forms and stuff. At home, I don't really have claimants waiting on me to send them things, so it's not as big of a deal.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school, did you have similar problems with attention and fidgeting that were causing you real trouble back then, or did things seem to get noticeably worse after you started working?\n\nPatient: Um, well, looking back, I guess it's not exactly a new thing for me, honestly. Even way before high school, like back in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I was always losing my worksheets or missing instructions. They were constantly having to remind me to finish my classwork. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was always getting on me about my messy desk. Her classroom always smelled strongly of peppermint, because she kept this huge glass jar of peppermints on her desk. She said they helped with digestion, but I think she just really liked them. She'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I was terrible at spelling, though. I could never remember if it was \"i before e\" or \"e before i.\" English has so many weird rules that contradict themselves. Like, why is \"colonel\" spelled with an \"l\" but pronounced with an \"r\"? It makes no sense. I tried looking it up once, and it has something to do with the French and the Italians arguing over military terms hundreds of years ago. History is full of stuff like that, just people arguing and messing things up for the rest of us. \n\nBut yeah, I guess I've always kind of struggled with missing things and needing reminders, even back then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, no, not really. I don't remember anyone ever saying I was hyper or anything like that. I mean, I wasn't the kid bouncing off the walls or running around when I wasn't supposed to. I was pretty quiet, actually. I just sort of drifted off a lot. 

I remember in middle school, we had this science teacher, Mr. Henderson, who had this massive fish tank in the back of the room. It took up like half the wall. He had all these exotic fish in there, like these bright blue ones that would dart around, and this one ugly bottom-feeder that just sucked on the glass all day. I used to stare at that tank for entire periods. I'd just watch the bubbles from the filter going up and up. It was so mesmerizing. Mr. Henderson was really into aquariums; he even had a whole separate tank just for breeding brine shrimp to feed the bigger fish. He said it was cheaper than buying fish food, but honestly, it smelled terrible. The whole back of the classroom always smelled like low tide. I don't know how the kids who sat back there stood it. I sat near the front, mostly because my parents asked the teachers to put me there so I'd pay more attention. It didn't really work, though, because I could still see the fish tank perfectly fine from the second row. 

But yeah, to answer your question, no, nobody ever told me I couldn't sit still or that I moved around too much. It was just the spacing out and losing stuff.

I work in insurance claims, you know, processing policies and stuff, and it's just... I don't know, I get up, and then I'm in the breakroom, and someone brought donuts the other day. Glazed ones, from that place on 4th street? They're so good, but they always run out of the chocolate frosted ones first. Anyway, my supervisor is starting to notice I'm not at my desk when I should be.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting at your desk trying to do your work, do you find it hard to keep your attention on the task itself, or is it more that you feel restless and need to get up and move around?\n\nPatient: Um, I think it's more that my attention just... slides right off what I'm doing. Like, I'll be staring at a file, and if the people in the next cubicle start talking about another claim, or if I just suddenly remember some random errand I have to do after work... like picking up my dry cleaning. Actually, I need to find a new dry cleaner because the one I go to shrunk my favorite sweater last week. It was this really nice wool one my mom got me for Christmas, and now it could probably fit a toddler. It's such a hassle to find a place that actually reads the care labels, you know? But anyway, yeah, I'll get distracted by a thought like that, or my coworkers talking, and my focus is just gone. I end up having to reread the exact same section of the claim a few times before it actually registers in my brain.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these problems with attention and getting up from your desk? Have they been going on for most of your life, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: Um, well, the work stuff specifically... that really started about 15 months ago? Yeah, because that’s when they did this huge department reorganization. They moved our whole team from the third floor down to the second floor, which honestly sucks because the second floor doesn't get nearly as much natural light. I used to have this little potted plant on my desk—a pothos, I think? My roommate gave it to me when we first signed our lease. \n\nHe’s actually moving out next month, though, because his girlfriend got a job in Seattle, so now I have to find a new roommate. It is just the absolute worst process. People on the internet are so weird, you know? Like, one guy asked if I was okay with him practicing his bagpipes at 6 AM. I can barely wake up to my normal alarm, let alone bagpipes. Anyway, the plant completely died because of the new office lighting. \n\nBut wait, what was I... oh, right. Yeah, ever since that reorganization 15 months ago, that's when I really started noticing how bad I was getting with the zoning out and wandering away from my desk.\n\nDoctor: Before that reorganization 15 months ago, did you have trouble focusing or staying in your seat at work, or in school before that? Or is this really the first time you've dealt with this kind of thing?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess if I really think about it... no, it's not a brand new thing. Like, looking back at elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards all the time that I was a bright kid, but I was just constantly losing my worksheets. \n\nI remember this one time in fourth grade, Mr. Henderson's class—he was obsessed with dinosaurs and had this huge T-Rex skull replica on his desk that we weren't allowed to touch. Which was honestly so unfair because why put it right there in front of us if we can't even touch it, right? He said it was fragile, but I'm pretty sure it was just cheap plastic. He bought it at a museum gift shop on a road trip, I think. I love road trips, actually, except for the fast food. You eat burgers for three days straight and you just feel terrible. \n\nBut anyway, I'd always lose his spelling packets, or I'd just completely miss the instructions when he was explaining what we were supposed to do. My mom actually tried color-coding my folders to help me keep track of things. Red for math, blue for reading... I think green was science? Or maybe green was social studies. I always hated social studies because we had to memorize all the state capitals, and I still don't know why I need to know the capital of North Dakota. Bismarck, maybe? \n\nBut yeah, my teachers always had to come over to my desk and give me constant reminders to actually finish my classwork instead of just sitting there. So I guess the missing instructions and needing someone to remind me to finish my work has kind of always been a thing.\n\nDoctor: Besides getting up from your desk at work, do you find it hard to sit still in general? Like, when you're watching TV or in meetings, do you feel like you need to be moving or fidgeting with something?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, sitting still is definitely a struggle. Like, if we have one of those longer team meetings or a training session, I'm pretty much always bouncing my leg or clicking my pen. Or just constantly shifting around in my chair, even when I'm really trying to look like I'm paying attention. The chairs in our conference room are the worst, by the way. They have this mesh back that's supposed to be ergonomic or whatever, but it just pinches you if you lean back too far. I don't know who picked them out, probably the same person who decided the second floor was a good place for our department. \n\nI actually tried bringing in my own cushion once, one of those memory foam ones? But I left it in the breakroom by accident and when I went back for it, it was gone. Who steals a seat cushion? It's just weird. It reminds me of when someone kept stealing my yogurts from the fridge at my old apartment. It was my roommate's boyfriend, I'm pretty sure, because he was always there and he ate constantly. He was a personal trainer, so he was always talking about his macros. Macros this, macros that... it was exhausting to listen to. \n\nBut yeah, what were we talking about? Oh, right, fidgeting. Yeah, clicking pens, bouncing my leg, shifting around... I do all that stuff pretty much anytime I have to sit in a meeting or a training for more than a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school, like in first, second, or third grade, do you remember your parents or teachers being concerned about your attention or activity level back then, or did those issues really only start showing up later, like in middle school or high school?\n\nPatient: Um, well, like I was saying before, it definitely wasn't a brand new thing that just popped up later on. Even way back in elementary school, like first, second, third grade... my teachers were already noticing stuff. They'd always write on my report cards that I was a bright kid, but I was constantly losing my worksheets or missing instructions. \n\nI actually still have some of those old report cards in a box somewhere at my parents' house. My mom kept everything. She has this huge plastic bin in the attic just full of my old macaroni art and spelling tests. The attic gets so hot in the summer, though, I'm surprised the macaroni hasn't completely melted or whatever macaroni does when it gets too hot. We had a squirrel get up there once, which was a whole nightmare. My dad tried to catch it himself with a Havahart trap, but he ended up just catching the neighbor's cat. Twice. The cat's name was Barnaby, he was this huge orange tabby who always looked angry. I don't really blame him for being angry if he kept getting stuck in a trap in our attic. \n\nBut yeah, anyway... the teachers were definitely concerned back then. They always had to give me reminders to actually finish my classwork because I'd just be sitting there not doing it. So it's definitely been going on since I was little.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over your whole life—school, work, relationships, hobbies—have these attention and fidgeting issues actually caused real problems for you, or have you mostly been able to manage okay despite them?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah, they've definitely caused real problems. Like right now, at work, it's actually getting pretty bad. My supervisor just put me on this performance coaching plan because my claim files are falling way behind. And she's questioning my accuracy, which is super frustrating because I'm usually pretty good at catching details, just... not lately, I guess. \n\nIt really sucks because I was aiming for the senior adjuster track, and now I'm actually worried I might lose that opportunity completely. Senior adjusters get their own cubicles by the windows, which is huge because right now I'm in the middle row where the fluorescent lights just buzz all day. It's this high-pitched hum that nobody else seems to hear, but it drives me crazy. I brought it up to maintenance, and this guy Gary came up to check it. Gary's nice, he always wears these vintage bowling shirts, like every single day. I asked him where he gets them, and he said thrift stores, mostly, but you have to go on Tuesdays when they put the new stock out. I tried going to a thrift store on a Tuesday once, but I ended up just buying a bunch of old vinyl records I don't even have a player for. I just liked the album covers. The artwork from the 70s is so weird and cool, you know? Like, there's always a wizard or a spaceship or something. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the performance coaching thing. It's definitely a problem right now. If I don't get my numbers up and fix the accuracy issues, I don't know what's going to happen with the promotion.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been evaluated by a doctor or psychiatrist before for attention problems or ADHD, or has anyone ever given you a diagnosis related to your attention or activity level?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I've never actually been evaluated for anything like this before. No doctor or anyone has ever given me a diagnosis or anything. I mean, my mom used to joke about it when I was younger, but we never actually went to a doctor. \n\nHonestly, going to the doctor was always a huge ordeal when I was a kid anyway. My pediatrician's office was in this massive medical complex, and the parking garage was basically a labyrinth. My mom would always lose the ticket, or forget what level we parked on. Once, we spent like forty-five minutes wandering around level four, looking for our minivan. It was a Dodge Caravan, one of those weird teal colors they used to make in the 90s. We called it the \"Teal Turtle\" because it was so incredibly slow going uphill. We took it on a road trip to the Grand Canyon once, and I swear I thought we were going to start rolling backward when we were driving up some of those mountain passes. My sister got carsick on that trip, actually. She always got carsick, even on short trips to the grocery store. She had to sit in the front seat with the window down, which meant I was stuck in the back with all the luggage and the cooler. The cooler was always packed with these little juice boxes that were impossible to open without squirting juice everywhere. \n\nBut yeah, no, to answer your question, I've never been officially evaluated or diagnosed with anything before this. This is the first time I'm really looking into it.\n\nDoctor: Besides the work performance issues you mentioned, have these attention and fidgeting problems caused you real difficulties in other areas of your life—like in your relationships, your social life, or managing everyday tasks at home?\n\nPatient: Um, well, let's see... relationships and everyday tasks... I mean, I don't really have a lot to say about that. I haven't really noticed it causing huge problems with my friends or at home, or at least I'm not sure if it does. I mostly just hang out with a couple of guys from high school anyway. We usually play video games online on Thursday nights. We've been playing this one game, Destiny 2, for like years now. It's crazy how much money they expect you to spend on expansions, though. Every time you turn around, it's another forty bucks just to get the new weapons and the new storyline. And the storyline doesn't even make sense half the time anymore. They killed off my favorite character a few years ago and I'm still kind of mad about it. His name was Cayde-6, he was this robot guy with a horn on his head, and he was hilarious. The guy who voiced him is that actor from Firefly, Nathan Fillion. I loved Firefly. It's a crime they cancelled it after one season. My buddy Dave actually has a replica of the spaceship from that show sitting on his desk. Dave's a cool guy, but he's terrible at Destiny. He always rushes in and dies immediately, and then we have to revive him while getting shot at. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know, I haven't really noticed major problems outside of work. Maybe I just don't pay enough attention to it, but I don't really have anything specific to tell you about that stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you think about managing things at home—like paying bills on time, keeping your apartment clean, remembering appointments, or keeping up with responsibilities—do you find yourself struggling with those kinds of things the way you struggle at work, or do those seem to go more smoothly for you?\n\nPatient: Um, paying bills and stuff... I mean, mostly that's okay, I guess? Everything's on autopay now, which is a lifesaver. But honestly, the forgetting stuff happens way more at work than at home. Like, I forget simple follow-ups way more than I want to admit. I'll get off the phone with a claimant and mean to return their call later with an answer, or I need to update a diary note on a file, or send out a form I told them I'd send... and it just totally slips my mind. \n\nIt's like I have the thought, and then it's just gone. Like when you walk into a room and forget why you went in there. My apartment layout makes that happen all the time. It's this weird shotgun style where you have to walk through the bedroom to get to the bathroom, and the kitchen is sort of just a wall in the living room. The fridge is right next to the front door, which makes bringing groceries in easy, but it's super loud. It makes this weird clunking noise right before the compressor kicks on. I actually tried to record the sound once to play for the landlord, but my phone microphone wouldn't pick it up right. I have one of those older phones, the ones with the physical home button still. I refuse to upgrade because I hate the idea of not having a real button to press. Plus, the new ones don't even come with a charger in the box anymore, which feels like a total scam. You pay a thousand dollars for a phone and they can't even give you a little plastic brick to plug it into the wall? It's ridiculous. \n\nBut yeah, work is where the forgetting really gets me in trouble. The follow-ups and the forms and stuff. At home, I don't really have claimants waiting on me to send them things, so it's not as big of a deal.\n\nDoctor: When you were in high school, did you have similar problems with attention and fidgeting that were causing you real trouble back then, or did things seem to get noticeably worse after you started working?\n\nPatient: Um, well, looking back, I guess it's not exactly a new thing for me, honestly. Even way before high school, like back in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I was always losing my worksheets or missing instructions. They were constantly having to remind me to finish my classwork. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was always getting on me about my messy desk. Her classroom always smelled strongly of peppermint, because she kept this huge glass jar of peppermints on her desk. She said they helped with digestion, but I think she just really liked them. She'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I was terrible at spelling, though. I could never remember if it was \"i before e\" or \"e before i.\" English has so many weird rules that contradict themselves. Like, why is \"colonel\" spelled with an \"l\" but pronounced with an \"r\"? It makes no sense. I tried looking it up once, and it has something to do with the French and the Italians arguing over military terms hundreds of years ago. History is full of stuff like that, just people arguing and messing things up for the rest of us. \n\nBut yeah, I guess I've always kind of struggled with missing things and needing reminders, even back then. High school wasn't much different, really.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school or high school, besides losing things and having trouble focusing, do you remember being told by teachers or your parents that you were hyperactive, or that you moved around too much, or that you couldn't sit still in class?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't remember anyone ever saying I was hyper or anything like that. I mean, I wasn't the kid bouncing off the walls or running around when I wasn't supposed to. I was pretty quiet, actually. I just sort of drifted off a lot. \n\nI remember in middle school, we had this science teacher, Mr. Henderson, who had this massive fish tank in the back of the room. It took up like half the wall. He had all these exotic fish in there, like these bright blue ones that would dart around, and this one ugly bottom-feeder that just sucked on the glass all day. I used to stare at that tank for entire periods. I'd just watch the bubbles from the filter going up and up. It was so mesmerizing. Mr. Henderson was really into aquariums; he even had a whole separate tank just for breeding brine shrimp to feed the bigger fish. He said it was cheaper than buying fish food, but honestly, it smelled terrible. The whole back of the classroom always smelled like low tide. I don't know how the kids who sat back there stood it. I sat near the front, mostly because my parents asked the teachers to put me there so I'd pay more attention. It didn't really work, though, because I could still see the fish tank perfectly fine from the second row. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, no, nobody ever told me I couldn't sit still or that I moved around too much. It was just the spacing out and losing stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Even way before high school, like back in elementary school, my teachers used to write on my report cards that I was bright but I was always losing my worksheets or missing instructions. They were constantly having to remind me to finish my classwork. I had this one teacher in fourth grade, Mrs. Gable, she was always getting on me about my messy desk. Her classroom always smelled strongly of peppermint, because she kept this huge glass jar of peppermints on her desk. She said they helped with digestion, but I think she just really liked them. She'd give them out if you got a perfect score on a spelling test. I was terrible at spelling, though. I could never remember if it was \"i before e\" or \"e before i.\" English has so many weird rules that contradict themselves. Like, why is \"colonel\" spelled with an \"l\" but pronounced with an \"r\"? It makes no sense. I tried looking it up once, and it has something to do with the French and the Italians arguing over military terms hundreds of years ago. History is full of stuff like that, just people arguing and messing things up for the rest of us. \n\nBut yeah, I guess I've always kind of struggled with missing things and needing reminders, even back then. High school wasn't much different, really.\n\nDoctor: When you were in elementary school or high school, besides losing things and having trouble focusing, do you remember being told by teachers or your parents that you were hyperactive, or that you moved around too much, or that you couldn't sit still in class?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't remember anyone ever saying I was hyper or anything like that. I mean, I wasn't the kid bouncing off the walls or running around when I wasn't supposed to. I was pretty quiet, actually. I just sort of drifted off a lot. \n\nI remember in middle school, we had this science teacher, Mr. Henderson, who had this massive fish tank in the back of the room. It took up like half the wall. He had all these exotic fish in there, like these bright blue ones that would dart around, and this one ugly bottom-feeder that just sucked on the glass all day. I used to stare at that tank for entire periods. I'd just watch the bubbles from the filter going up and up. It was so mesmerizing. Mr. Henderson was really into aquariums; he even had a whole separate tank just for breeding brine shrimp to feed the bigger fish. He said it was cheaper than buying fish food, but honestly, it smelled terrible. The whole back of the classroom always smelled like low tide. I don't know how the kids who sat back there stood it. I sat near the front, mostly because my parents asked the teachers to put me there so I'd pay more attention. It didn't really work, though, because I could still see the fish tank perfectly fine from the second row. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, no, nobody ever told me I couldn't sit still or that I moved around too much. It was just the spacing out and losing stuff.\n\n[Last Question]\nSo just to be clear—when you were a kid, you weren't the type to interrupt people a lot, or blurt things out, or act without thinking first, right? It was really just the attention and losing things that were the main issues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly. I wasn't really the disruptive kid at all. I wasn't blurting out answers or talking out of turn. I mean, I barely raised my hand even when I did know the answer. I was just really quiet. 

My mom actually used to worry that I was too quiet. She tried to get me into all these extracurriculars to bring me out of my shell. She signed me up for Little League when I was like eight, but I was terrible at baseball. I played right field, which is basically where they put the kids who can't catch. I'd just stand out there pulling up grass and making little piles of it. I found a four-leaf clover once during a game. I put it in my pocket to save it, but I forgot about it and it went through the wash. My mom was so mad because the green stain wouldn't come out of the white baseball pants. Those pants were impossible to keep clean anyway. I don't know who decided kids should wear white pants to slide around in the dirt. It's like a conspiracy by the laundry detergent companies, I swear. They probably sponsor the leagues just so parents have to buy more stain remover. 

But no, I wasn't interrupting or acting crazy. I was just in my own little world most of the time. The teachers mostly just said I needed to apply myself more, you know?
